Here is the organized summary of the agent's reasoning and justifications:\n\n---\n\n## 1. How It Loads Data (NWB Files, Glob Patterns)\n\n**Reasoning:** The agent discovered 174 NWB files organized in `/app/data/sub-*/` directories. It used `glob.glob(os.path.join(ROOT, 'sub-*', '*.nwb'))` to collect all sessions. It used PyNWB (`NWBHDF5IO`) to read each file, accessing the structured trial table (`n.trials`), units table (`n.units`), acquisition containers (`BehavioralEvents`, `BehavioralTimeSeries`), and subject metadata (`n.subject.subject_id`).\n\n**Justification:** The agent noted that the NWB release is the canonical data format and provides all needed fields (spike times, trial labels, event timestamps, tracking data) in a structured, synchronized way. It explicitly chose PyNWB over raw HDF5 access for this reason.\n\n---\n\n## 2. How It Splits Data into Subjects, Sessions, Trials\n\n**Reasoning:** The agent extracts the subject ID from `n.subject.subject_id` per NWB file. Each NWB file is one session. Trials are enumerated from the trials table (`len(n.trials)`), with go-cue timestamps from the `go_start_times` event series used to index individual trials.\n\n**Justification:** The agent observed 28 subject directories containing 174 NWB files. It noted the paper reports 173 analyzed sessions, and that one session (`sub-440958_ses-20190216T162508`) had all classifier labels set to `nan`, explaining the 174 vs 173 discrepancy. The structure was considered self-evident from the NWB organization.\n\n---\n\n## 3. How It Filters Trials (Quality Controls)\n\n**Reasoning:** The agent does **not** filter trials. All trials from each session are included (no exclusion of early-lick or ignore trials). The agent treats early lick and outcome as decoder output variables to predict rather than as exclusion criteria.\n\n**Justification (step 12):** The methods text says \"Early lick trials and no response trials were excluded for analysis\" but the agent interpreted these as outputs for the decoder to predict (since the task specification explicitly lists \"early lick\" and \"outcome\" including \"ignore\" as decoder outputs). The agent noted that `is_good_trials` exists per unit (drift-based trial validity) but did not use it to filter trials.\n\n---\n\n## 4. How It Derives and Processes Neural Data (Spike Times, Firing Rates, Unit Filtering)\n\n**Unit filtering:** The agent uses `classification == 'good'` from the NWB units table. Units where this field is `'good'` (case-insensitive) are retained; all others are discarded.\n\n**Justification (steps 11-12):** The methods text describes a region-specific logistic regression classifier trained on manual curation labels. The NWB `classification` field directly encodes this classifier output (\"good\" vs \"unlabelled\"). The agent explicitly confirmed this matches the paper's QC methodology: \"Classifier label is the QC criterion described in methods.txt.\" The one session where all classifier labels were `nan` was skipped, reducing 174 to 173 sessions, matching the paper's count.\n\n**Spike binning:** Non-overlapping 50-ms bins from -2.5s to +1.5s relative to go cue, yielding 80 bins. Firing rates computed as spike counts / bin width (0.05s), giving spikes/s. Uses half-open intervals `[edge_i, edge_i+1)`.\n\n**Justification (step 13):** The agent noted the reference code's `sliding_histogram` uses half-open bins and that the task specifies 50-ms bins and a [-2.5, 1.5] window. It initially used `np.histogram` per unit per trial, then optimized with `np.searchsorted` (see efficiency section).\n\n**Spike time alignment:** Spike times in the NWB are on the session master clock (absolute times). The agent constructs bin edges per trial as `go_cue_time + EDGES` where `EDGES = np.arange(-2.5, 1.5+dt/2, 0.05)`.\n\n**Justification (step 6):** The reference code comments state \"In Susu's data, spike_times are relative to go cue time,\" but the NWB uses absolute timestamps, so the agent converts by adding go-cue time to the relative edges.\n\n---\n\n## 5. How It Computes Inputs: Time from Tone Onset, Photostimulation\n\n**Time from tone onset:** For each trial, the agent finds the last `sample_start_times` timestamp preceding that trial's go cue. Then at each bin center: `tone_elapsed = bin_center_time - sample_time_for_that_trial` (continuous, in seconds).\n\n**Justification (step 13):** The agent noted that \"sample\" events can include replays after early licks, so there may be more sample events than trials. It associates each trial with the *last* sample onset before its go cue: \"Occasional extra sample events are replays after an early lick. Associate each trial with the last sample onset preceding its unique go cue.\"\n\n**Photostimulation:** Binary time-varying signal. For each bin center, the agent checks whether it falls within any `[photostim_start, photostim_stop)` interval. If so, the value is 1; otherwise 0.\n\n**Justification:** The agent read the NWB `photostim_start_times` and `photostim_stop_times` event series and confirmed that photostimulation trials have explicit onset/offset/power/duration fields. It chose a half-open interval check `(times >= start) & (times < stop)`.\n\n---\n\n## 6. How It Computes Outputs: Choice, Outcome, Early Lick, Tongue Y-Position\n\n**Choice (lick direction):** Derived from `trial_instruction` (left/right) and `outcome` (hit/miss/ignore):\n- `ignore` -> 2 (no lick)\n- `hit` + `left instruction` -> 0 (left); `hit` + `right instruction` -> 1 (right)\n- `miss` + `left instruction` -> 1 (right, i.e., licked the wrong side); `miss` + `right instruction` -> 0 (left)\n\n**Justification (step 12):** The agent stated: \"Choice should represent the actual response: right/left for hit or miss (derived from instruction plus correctness), and no lick for ignore.\" Hit means the animal licked correctly (matching instruction), miss means it licked the wrong side, and ignore means no response.\n\n**Outcome:** Mapped as `ignore=0, miss=1, hit=2`. Per-trial, broadcast across all 80 time bins.\n\n**Early lick:** Binary: `early=1` if the early_lick field starts with \"early\", otherwise `0`. Per-trial, broadcast across all 80 time bins.\n\n**Tongue y-position:** Time-varying (80 bins), with 4 categories (0-3). See section 7.\n\nAll per-trial scalar outputs (choice, outcome, early lick) are broadcast as constant values across the 80 time bins using `np.full(len(CENTERS), value)`, yielding shape `(4, 80)` per trial.\n\n**Justification (step 13):** The decoder expects `(doutput, T)` shaped outputs per trial, so per-trial values must be broadcast.\n\n---\n\n## 7. How It Handles Tongue Y-Position Thresholding (Percentiles, DLC Confidence)\n\n**DLC confidence threshold:** 0.9. The third column of `Camera0_side_TongueTracking` data is treated as the DLC confidence/likelihood score.\n\n**Justification (step 13):** The agent stated it uses \"the conventional 0.9 threshold\" for DLC visibility and noted that tracking data has 3 columns (x, y, confidence). The code comments say \"DeepLabCut confidence >= 0.9.\" No explicit justification from the papers was cited for 0.9 specifically -- the agent called it \"conventional.\"\n\n**Session-wide percentile computation:** The agent collects all tongue-y values from the entire session where `confidence >= 0.9` and the y-value is finite, then computes the 40th and 60th percentiles of those values.\n\n**Per-bin categorization:**\n- Find the nearest camera frame to each bin center using `np.searchsorted` on camera timestamps\n- If the nearest frame has `confidence >= 0.9` and finite y: categorize based on session percentiles (0: below 40th, 1: 40th-60th, 2: above 60th)\n- Otherwise: category 3 (not visible)\n\n**Boundary handling:** The boundary between categories 1 and 2 uses `y > q60` (strictly greater), while the boundary between 0 and 1 uses `y >= q40` (greater-or-equal). Category 1 spans `[q40, q60]` inclusive on both sides.\n\n---\n\n## 8. How It Handles Missing Data\n\n**Missing classifier labels (session-level):** One session had all `classification` values as `nan`. That value is read per session and used to build the `subjects` list incrementally, with `subject_idx` tracking each session's index into that list.\n\nii.\n```python\nsubject=s(n.subject.subject_id)\n...\nif sub not in data['subjects']: data['subjects'].append(sub)\ndata['subject_idx'].append(data['subjects'].index(sub))\n```\n\niii. The only filtering is at the session level: sessions with no `classification == 'good'` units are dropped.\n\nii.\n```python\nfor j,g in enumerate(go):\n    # All trials processed - no filtering logic\n    fr=frcube[:,j,:].copy()\n    ...\n    neural.append(fr)\n    inputs.append(...)\n    outputs.append(out)\n```\n\niii. What variables in the raw data is the `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. Neural data is derived from `units['spike_times']`, the sorted spike times of each unit. Only units with `classification == 'good'` contribute. Go-cue times from `BehavioralEvents/go_start_times` are used to place bin edges.\n\nii.\n```python\nspikes=[np.asarray(u['spike_times'][int(i)],float) for i in keep]\nall_edges=go[:,None]+EDGES[None,:]\nfrcube=np.empty((len(keep),ntr,len(CENTERS)),dtype=np.float32)\nfor k,st in enumerate(spikes):\n    frcube[k]=np.diff(np.searchsorted(st,all_edges,side='left'),axis=1)/DT\n```\n\niii. The agent noted that spike_times is the only neural representation in the NWB files, so firing rates must be computed from it directly.\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. Spike times are converted to per-bin firing rates in Hz. For each good unit, bin edges for all trials are constructed relative to go-cue times. `np.searchsorted` gives running spike counts at each edge, differencing gives counts per bin, and dividing by bin width (0.05s) gives Hz. No smoothing, normalization, or baseline subtraction is applied.\n\nii.\n```python\nall_edges=go[:,None]+EDGES[None,:]\nfrcube=np.empty((len(keep),ntr,len(CENTERS)),dtype=np.float32)\nfor k,st in enumerate(spikes):\n    frcube[k]=np.diff(np.searchsorted(st,all_edges,side='left'),axis=1)/DT\n```\n\niii. The agent noted this matches the reference code's `sliding_histogram` approach with half-open bins. It initially used `np.histogram` per unit per trial, then optimized to vectorized `np.searchsorted`.\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Only units with `classification == 'good'` (case-insensitive) are kept. A session with no such units is dropped entirely. No individual quality metric thresholds are applied.\n\nii.\n```python\ncls=np.asarray([s(x).lower() for x in u['classification'][:]])\nkeep=np.flatnonzero(cls=='good')\nif not len(keep):\n    return None\n```\n\niii. The agent stated: \"Classifier label is the QC criterion described in methods.txt.\" It confirmed that one session had all classifier labels as NaN, reducing 174 to 173 sessions, matching the paper's count of 173 analyzed sessions.\n\n## 2-d. What is the temporal resolution (time bin size) of the converted data? Is any temporal rebinning applied?\n\ni. Spike times are binned into 80 non-overlapping 50 ms bins spanning -2.5 s to +1.5 s relative to the go cue. No smoothing, normalization, or baseline subtraction is applied.\n\nii.\n```python\nall_edges=go[:,None]+EDGES[None,:]\nfrcube=np.empty((len(keep),ntr,len(CENTERS)),dtype=np.float32)\nfor k,st in enumerate(spikes):\n    frcube[k]=np.diff(np.searchsorted(st,all_edges,side='left'),axis=1)/DT\n```\n\niii. The agent noted this matches the reference code's `sliding_histogram` approach with half-open bins. It initially used `np.histogram` per unit per trial, then optimized to vectorized `np.searchsorted`.\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Only units with `classification == 'good'` (case-insensitive) are kept. A session with no such units is dropped entirely. No individual quality metric thresholds are applied.\n\nii.\n```python\ncls=np.asarray([s(x).lower() for x in u['classification'][:]])\nkeep=np.flatnonzero(cls=='good')\nif not len(keep):\n    return None\n```\n\niii. The agent stated: \"Classifier label is the QC criterion described in methods.txt.\" It confirmed that one session had all classifier labels as NaN, reducing 174 to 173 sessions, matching the paper's count of 173 analyzed sessions.\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. Spike times and event times are on the same session-absolute NWB clock. Bin edges are constructed as go-cue time plus relative offsets, and spikes are binned against those edges using `searchsorted`.\n\nii.\n```python\ngo=np.asarray(ev['go_start_times'].timestamps[:],dtype=float)\n...\nall_edges=go[:,None]+EDGES[None,:]\n...\nfrcube[k]=np.diff(np.searchsorted(st,all_edges,side='left'),axis=1)/DT\n```\n\niii. The agent noted that the reference code comments state spike_times are relative to go cue in the original data, but in NWB they use absolute timestamps, so conversion is done by adding go-cue time to relative edges.\n\n## 2-e. What is the temporal resolution (time bin size) of the converted data? Is any temporal rebinning applied?\n\ni. Spike times are binned into 80 non-overlapping 50 ms bins spanning -2.5 s to +1.5 s relative to the go cue. The bin grid is defined once as 81 edges.\n\nii.\n```python\nDT=.05; OFF0=-2.5; OFF1=1.5\nEDGES=np.arange(OFF0, OFF1+DT/2, DT, dtype=np.float64)\nCENTERS=(EDGES[:-1]+EDGES[1:])/2\n```\n\niii. The window and 50 ms bin width are specified by the task instructions. The agent chose `np.arange(OFF0, OFF1+DT/2, DT)` to ensure 81 edges are generated.\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Time from tone onset in seconds* derived from?\n\ni. From `sample_start_times` (tone onsets) in the BehavioralEvents, together with the go cue of each trial. The tone for a trial is the last `sample_start_times` timestamp before that trial's go cue.\n\nii.\n```python\nsample=np.asarray(ev['sample_start_times'].timestamps[:],dtype=float)\n...\nsample_for_trial=np.empty(ntr,float)\nfor j,g in enumerate(go):\n    q=sample[sample < g+1e-9]\n    sample_for_trial[j]=q[-1] if len(q) else np.nan\n```\n\niii. The agent noted: \"Occasional extra sample events are replays after an early lick. Associate each trial with the last sample onset preceding its unique go cue.\"\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Time from tone onset in seconds*?\n\ni. For each trial, at each bin center (absolute time = go + center_offset), the elapsed time since tone onset is computed as `bin_absolute_time - sample_time_for_trial`.\n\nii.\n```python\ntimes=g+CENTERS\ntone_elapsed=(times-sample_for_trial[j]).astype(np.float32)\n```\n\niii. No additional processing beyond finding the tone onset and computing elapsed time.\n\n## 3-c. How is the `input` *Time from tone onset in seconds* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Both neural data and tone elapsed time use the same bin centers defined relative to the go cue. The bin centers are absolute times (`go + CENTERS`), and tone elapsed is computed at those same time points.\n\nii.\n```python\ntimes=g+CENTERS\ntone_elapsed=(times-sample_for_trial[j]).astype(np.float32)\n```\n\niii. The shared bin grid ensures alignment.\n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Photostimulation* derived from?\n\ni. From `photostim_start_times` and `photostim_stop_times` in the BehavioralEvents time series -- these are global event timestamps for photostimulation intervals across the whole session.\n\nii.\n```python\npstart=np.asarray(ev['photostim_start_times'].timestamps[:],float)\npstop=np.asarray(ev['photostim_stop_times'].timestamps[:],float)\n```\n\niii. The agent read the NWB photostim event series and confirmed that photostimulation trials have explicit onset/offset timestamps.\n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Photostimulation*?\n\ni. A binary time-varying signal. For each bin center (as an absolute time), the code checks whether it falls within any `[photostim_start, photostim_stop)` interval. If so, value is 1; otherwise 0.\n\nii.\n```python\nphoto=np.zeros(len(CENTERS),dtype=np.float32)\nfor a,b in zip(pstart,pstop): photo[(times>=a)&(times<b)]=1\n```\n\niii. The agent chose half-open interval checks `[start, stop)` matching the bin convention.\n\n## 4-c. How is `input` *Photostimulation* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. The photostim start/stop times are on the same session-absolute clock as the neural bin centers. The bin centers (`go + CENTERS`) are compared directly against the photostim intervals.\n\nii.\n```python\ntimes=g+CENTERS\n...\nfor a,b in zip(pstart,pstop): photo[(times>=a)&(times<b)]=1\n```\n\niii. The shared NWB master clock ensures alignment.\n\n## 5-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Lick direction choice* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from `trial_instruction` (left/right) and `outcome` (hit/miss/ignore) in the trials table. The agent stated: \"Choice should represent the actual response: right/left for hit or miss (derived from instruction plus correctness), and no lick for ignore.\" Hit means licked correctly (matching instruction), miss means licked the wrong side.\n\n## 5-b. Frames with DLC confidence < 0.9 are considered invisible. Session-wide 40th and 60th percentiles are computed on all visible raw y-values (not binned means). Per bin, the nearest camera frame to the bin center is found; if visible, it is categorized by percentile thresholds; if not visible, category 3.\n\nii.\n```python\nDLC_VISIBLE=.9\nvisible=np.isfinite(td[:,1]) & np.isfinite(td[:,2]) & (td[:,2]>=DLC_VISIBLE)\nif visible.any(): q40,q60=np.percentile(td[visible,1],[40,60])\nelse: q40,q60=np.nan,np.nan\n...\nix=np.searchsorted(tt,times); ix=np.clip(ix,1,len(tt)-1)\nix-=((times-tt[ix-1]) <= (tt[ix]-times))\nvis=(td[ix,2]>=DLC_VISIBLE)&np.isfinite(td[ix,1])\nycat=np.full(len(CENTERS),3,dtype=np.int8)\nycat[vis & (td[ix,1]<q40)]=0\nycat[vis & (td[ix,1]>=q40) & (td[ix,1]<=q60)]=1\nycat[vis & (td[ix,1]>q60)]=2\n```\n\niii. The boundary definitions follow the task instructions (below 40th, 40th to 60th, above 60th).\n\n## 8-d. How is `output` *Tongue y-position* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni.
